Reset has mattered more than ratings 
High reset spread AT1s have generally been more resilient in 2026, with the 700-800bp 
reset bucket even bucking the trend with some tightening over the last three months. 
While this relationship is not fully linear, and many high-reset bonds are issued by 
higher beta/lower rated issuers, numbers still suggest that reset levels have been an 
important driver of performance this year. Higher underlying rates reduce the cushion 
that lower reset AT1 bonds have had, as extension risk concerns have made something 
of a comeback in recent months. 
Higher reset levels also provide greater coupon protection if a bond is extended during a 
period of market stress. There are a few near-term low-reset bonds callable with reset 
levels below +380bp. However, extension risk is still low for these: longer-call AT1s from 
these issuers trade through the respective reset levels, and these banks have historically 
assessed calls holistically, rather than purely on an economics or bond-by-bond basis. 
Higher-rated bonds have underperformed in 2026. IG-rated bonds have not translated 
into better spread performance: BBB- rated AT1s now trade at similar spread levels as 
BB+ and BB-rated AT1s. 
Reset levels have been a better guide for performance than credit ratings in 2026 
High reset AT1s have been more resilient...  … while higher rated bonds have seen less love

Source: IHS Markit, ING  Source: IHS Markit, ING

So where does that leave the AT1 market in 2027? 
2027 is shaping up to be another interesting year for bank capital. Valuations look a 
bit compressed, but bank fundamentals remain solid, with banks likely to continue 
calling their instruments at the first call dates. In terms of supply, we expect the 
small increase to be absorbable but potentially lead to investors becoming a bit 
more price sensitive. Furthermore, the lack of imminent AT1 reform should mean 
the asset class retains an important role in Europe’s banking capital stacks for the 
foreseeable future. 
There are risks becoming more visible, not least through sovereign and fiscal 
concerns, with France the obvious victim at the moment. A softening of 
fundamentals at the margin and lower reset levels further along the call curve could 
create more volatility. Spreads have recently seen some widening, yet there is 
probably greater scope for spreads to stay wider than for further tightening in 2027. 
AT1 yields could remain a good driver of demand for investors, cushioning potential 
spread weakness. On the other hand, the market is likely to differentiate more 
between structures and issuer profiles, while remaining wary of banks with greater 
domestic sovereign sensitivity.

### Page 25

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Stablecoins are increasingly moving into the mainstream financial system as banks assess both the risks and 
opportunities they present

Stablecoins’ market capitalisation has grown significantly over the past two years. This 
growth has drawn widespread scrutiny, including from regulators, as it raises concerns 
about financial stability. Banks have also paid closer attention to the sector as it could 
represent both new risks and opportunities. 
In this piece, we review the potential impact stablecoins could have on the European 
banking sector. This is especially relevant as multiple regulatory changes are expected in 
2027. The EU is reviewing its Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCAR), which could 
affect the estimated impact of stablecoins on the banking sector. Across the Atlantic, 
the regulatory landscape could also change next year. Discussions could result in the US 
allowing stablecoin issuers to directly distribute rewards, which would be a first. 
Overall, while the future of stablecoins is still uncertain, they promise to remain in the 
spotlight in 2027. We show that despite multiple risks identified, European banks are 
unlikely to surrender ground to fintech stablecoin issuers without a fight. 
What are stablecoins? 
Stablecoins are crypto assets issued by private institutions that promise a stable 
nominal value in a given currency, commodity or pool of assets. Stablecoins are 
therefore not issued (or backed) by a government or central authority, but rather by a 
private institution and secured by segregated reserves. 
The crypto asset is issued on distributed ledger technology (DLT), such as a blockchain, 
with three existing types of pegging mechanisms. For the sake of this analysis, we will 
focus on the main one: fiat-collateralised stablecoins. These crypto assets are backed 
1:1 by reserves of cash or liquid assets. 
In our overview publication on stablecoins (read more here), we review the sector’s 
recent development. We show that the total market capitalisation of stablecoins is

Stablecoins’ growth – opportunity or 
threat for banks? 
While stablecoin adoption could significantly affect European banks, including by shifting 
their funding base, the sector could also benefit from the technology by issuing its own 
tokens. Upcoming regulatory changes could alter the impact of stablecoins on banks, but 
we should not expect banks to surrender ground to fintech issuers without a fight

### Page 26

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

surpassing $300bn globally, 99% of which consists of USD-pegged tokens. While still 
dwarfed by, for example, bank deposits, the rapid growth of the sector over the past five 
years (up by US$250bn) has drawn a lot of attention. 
Selected stablecoins' market capitalisation over time & share of all cryptocurrencies

USDT = Tether, USDC = Circle, USDS = Sky Protocol  
Source: ING Research, IMF

Growing interest in stablecoins stems both from possible efficiency gains and the 
potential risks they pose. For stablecoins to reach the ‘broad adoption’ level of 
development, usage should extend beyond the niche crypto landscape and into the 
payments world. Several variables, including regulation, integration into payment 
systems and relative attractiveness compared with other payment systems, must be 
considered when determining the future growth of stablecoins. Read more on that in 
our piece Stablecoins 101: Uncertain growth but the stakes are high. 
Despite their uncertain future, we believe it's important to consider a scenario in which 
stablecoins become broadly adopted and systemically important as a means of 
payment. We identify multiple potential impacts of the crypto asset, including 
implications for emerging markets, interest rates, and monetary policy. In this piece, we 
will focus specifically on potential implications for the banking sector. 
Stablecoins impact on banks: Threat, opportunity, or both? 
In this section, we explore the potential implications of a broad use of stablecoins 
beyond the crypto landscape and into payment systems for banks. Despite US dollar 
stablecoins currently dominating the market, our main assumption is the widespread 
adoption of euro-denominated stablecoins in Europe. 
While stablecoins are often presented as a challenge for the banking sector, we review 
here five potential implications, not all of which are negative for financial institutions. 
A change in banks' funding 
To discuss the consequences of an uptake in stablecoin adoption on banks’ funding, two 
variables must be considered: 
1. Which assets are used to buy stablecoins 
2. What type of reserves are required by the regulation 
If most users acquire stablecoins by redeeming liquid investments, the impact for 
financial institutions is expected to remain limited. However, if EU citizens and 
corporates adopt EUR-denominated stablecoins using bank deposits, the impact on 
financial institutions could be substantially more significant. In this scenario, banks could 
face a net drop in their retail and corporate deposits and/or a shift in the composition of 
their funding base. 
1

### Page 27

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Importantly, the nature of banks’ funding change depends on the second key variable: 
the regulation in place, more specifically the reserve regime. Indeed, widespread 
adoption of the crypto asset with deposits could result in a drop in banks’ funding. 
However, under the EU’s MiCAR, stablecoin issuers are required to hold part of their 
reserves as deposits with European commercial banks. 
This implies that part of the amount flowing out of banks would ultimately come back to 
the sector as reserves from stablecoin issuers. Stablecoin adoption would therefore not 
translate into a net drop in funding for banks but rather in a shift in their funding base, 
where retail and corporate deposits would come back in wholesale form (more 
specifically as financial institution deposits). 
Illustration of banks' funding shift through stablecoins adoption using retail deposits*

*stylized example using MiCAR for systemic issuers 
Source: ING

This shift from retail to wholesale deposits is not without consequences. Wholesale 
deposits are known to be more volatile than retail ones and therefore represent a less 
stable funding source for banks. Additionally, capital requirements are higher for 
wholesale deposits, with, for example, higher run-off rates in the Liquidity Coverage 
Ratio (LCR) and lower available stable funding weights in the Net Stable Funding Ratio 
(NSFR). This reflects their greater volatility and related risks, making them more 
expensive for banks to hold. 
Greater reliance on wholesale deposits could also create risk management challenges 
with more complex asset-liability matching. The ease with which deposits can be 
switched into stablecoins could increase financial institutions’ exposure to economic 
cycles. 
To date, all main regulations in place ban stablecoin issuers from directly remunerating 
holders. Therefore, an environment in which interest rates are lowered (or even 
approach zero) would make holding stablecoins more attractive for users, as the 
difference with holding a deposit account is reduced. This could trigger sudden 
stablecoin adoption and a quick shift in banks’ funding. 
The opposite scenario could also pose challenges for banks. As interest rates increase, 
the attractiveness of holding stablecoins diminishes. This could lead to sudden 
redemption requests from stablecoin holders, forcing issuers to liquidate reserves held at 
banks. As a result, financial institutions could face an outflow of stablecoin issuers’ 
deposits with a simultaneous inflow of retail deposits, adding volatility to their funding 
profiles. 
In either case, these funding changes and substitution dynamics could make banks’ 
funding management more complex, costly and difficult to predict. However, as long

### Page 28

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

as regulation prevents stablecoin issuers from directly remunerating holders, the effect 
of economic cycles is expected to remain muted, as stablecoins only become attractive 
as a deposit-like instrument in the case of a very low interest rate environment. 
Additionally, the impact is likely to vary across the banking sector. Financial institutions 
with a large base of digitally active customers could face heightened pressure from 
widespread stablecoin adoption, as the barriers to switching from deposits to crypto 
assets are lower. Differences could also emerge between deposit categories. The impact 
is expected to be especially noticeable for accounts used as payment instruments, held 
by younger, digitally native customers who tend to be less relationship-driven or loyal to 
a specific bank and generally more willing to adopt new financial technologies. 
While these points explore a scenario in which EUR-denominated stablecoins are 
widespread, it’s important to note that the impact would be different if USD-
denominated stablecoins continue to dominate the market. In that case, a significant 
uptake would not translate into a funding shift for European banks but rather into a net 
outflow of deposits. 
Indeed, these funds could flow to US stablecoin issuers, which would place the safe 
assets as required under US regulation. In other words, this would translate into a net 
outflow of European deposits towards the US economy. Aside from the monetary policy 
impact, European banks would be required to turn to alternative and more expensive 
sources of funding to compensate for the outflow of retail deposits. 
However, EU banks can also play a central role in the issuance and adoption of EUR-
denominated stablecoins to counter that effect, as we discuss below. 
Pressure on credit provision 
In addition to changing banks’ funding base, broad stablecoin adoption could also 
negatively impact credit provision through several channels. 
In a scenario where banks see a funding shift towards greater reliance on wholesale 
deposits, higher liquidity requirements may push them to take more conservative risk 
decisions. Research has shown that this could translate into banks steering their balance 
sheets away from illiquid and long-maturity loans to ensure they comply with the 
regulation, as well as reducing loan volumes, shortening maturities and increasing loan 
pricing. Overall, the European Central Bank has shown that deposit substitution could 
lead to a contraction of about 0.2% in lending to firms. 
In addition to regulatory restrictions, wholesale deposits show different volatility 
patterns from those of retail deposits, mainly by being more reactive to market and 
macroeconomic changes. Greater reliance on such deposits would, once again, imply 
more complex risk management, pushing banks to hold additional liquidity buffers 
which could increase loan pricing. 
Here, again, the impact could differ depending on the financial institution. European 
banks already showing high loan-to-deposit ratios, as well as those close to regulatory 
thresholds, could be more inclined to pass higher funding costs on through lending 
conditions in order to remain within regulatory thresholds. 
Concentration and spillover risks 
A growing discussion among regulators centres on concentration risks. As stablecoin 
issuers are required to hold part of their reserves as deposits with commercial banks, 
financial institutions could increasingly rely on their deposits for funding. As the 
proportion of banks’ funding coming from a small number of stablecoin issuers 
increases, so does concentration risk. This impact may become substantial if stablecoin 
adoption is both significant and concentrated among a handful of issuers. 
2 
3

### Page 29

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Concentration could also arise if stablecoin issuers maintain relationships with only a 
limited number of banks, those with both the technical capabilities and willingness to 
engage with the sector. This could create pockets of concentration on both sides of the 
business, introducing new systemic risks for the banking sector. 
Regulators across the globe have been increasingly attentive to the development of 
stablecoins. However, as concentration risks between banks and stablecoin issuers 
potentially emerge, they might also see the need to update their frameworks to ensure 
that such risks are correctly accounted for and captured in stress testing. 
In addition, the current EU regulation also creates a direct link between the banking 
sector and crypto asset issuers, increasing spillover risks. In the event of a run on 
stablecoins, where the crypto asset suddenly loses value and users redeem their 
holdings, banks carrying the issuer’s reserves could face sudden and significant 
redemption requests. This could transmit a shock in the crypto sector to the broader 
financial system. The reverse is also true, with stablecoin issuers potentially affected by 
the failure of, or reputational damage to, the commercial bank they hold reserves with. 
These concerns promise to remain relevant in 2027 as the EU is reviewing its MiCA 
Regulation. In May 2026, the European Commission opened a consultation to assess 
whether the MiCAR remains ‘fit for purpose’ following its initial implementation and 
recent market developments. In September, the ECB, alongside the 27 EU national 
central banks, submitted a recommendation to drop the requirement for stablecoin 
issuers to hold deposits at commercial banks. 
Instead, the European System of Central Banks proposes implementing a minimum 
percentage of assets maturing within one to five working days to be held by the 
stablecoin issuer. This is said to lower both concentration and spillover risks. However, it 
could further accentuate the impact on banks’ funding by transforming a funding shift 
into a net deposit outflow. 
Stablecoin and, more generally, crypto asset regulations are still emerging globally. 
Other regulators have highlighted the risks that links between stablecoins and the 
banking sector could create. The most striking example comes from the UK, where the 
Bank of England announced that it aims to ban stablecoin issuers from holding reserves 
at commercial banks, specifically to mitigate spillover risks in case of a shock. As 
stablecoin usage continues to rise, we can expect further changes to the regulatory 
landscape globally. 
Competing through innovation 
Despite the lack of international harmonisation, current regulations restrict stablecoin 
issuers from directly distributing remuneration to holders. This represents a significant 
regulatory advantage for the banking sector, as it limits the use of the crypto asset to 
transactional purposes. Remuneration distribution is therefore a central discussion point 
for stablecoin issuers, as removing the ban would allow them to position their product 
not only as a means of payment but also as a store of value. 2027 will be a crucial year 
as negotiations surrounding the US Clarity Act continue, potentially allowing stablecoin 
issuers to directly distribute rewards. While still far from an agreement, such a 
derogation would be a first globally and could open the door for similar changes in other 
jurisdictions. To counter this competition, banks could increase interest rates on their 
deposit accounts; however, this would have an impact on their profitability. 
Therefore, the critical response to the rise of stablecoins lies instead in innovation. 
Indeed, crypto assets’ main advantage lies in their speed and limited costs, but banks 
are already showing they can innovate to compete. The European payment landscape 
is relatively cheap and fast despite being heavily fragmented. Some national payment 
systems exist, but a large share of transactions are processed through non-EU providers. 
4

### Page 30

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Stablecoins’ promise to process payments (nearly) instantly and at extremely low costs, 
even across borders, could put pressure on existing systems. However, banks and 
payment system providers are already exploring ways to compete and experimenting 
with new digital transaction methods. 
One example lies in tokenisation. Tokenised assets are digital representations of real-
world assets on a distributed ledger infrastructure. A wide range of assets can be 
tokenised, but most banks are experimenting with deposit tokenisation. These could 
modernise payment and settlement infrastructures as well as intragroup liquidity 
management. They promise faster settlement, 24/7 transactions, programmability and 
lower operational frictions. Additionally, tokenised deposits offer regulatory advantages, 
as they remain fully within the existing supervisory framework. The underlying assets 
stay on the commercial banks’ balance sheet, preserving the same oversight and 
prudential standards applied to traditional deposits. 
Banks’ competitive response could also extend to the regulatory field. The sector could 
push regulators to implement or maintain strict rules for the crypto asset sector to 
adequately reflect their risks. The US provides us with a striking example, as negotiations 
for the Clarity Act have been ongoing for months, with financial institutions pressing the 
importance of the ‘same risk, same regulation’ principle. While the final version of the US 
crypto landscape regulation is still to be agreed upon, US banks have played a central 
role in the negotiations by requesting proportionate safeguards surrounding the use of 
crypto assets. 
Banks' role in stablecoin adoption 
While we have explored in depth the impact stablecoins could have on the banking 
sector upon broad adoption, it’s important to highlight that banks also play a role in 
the crypto asset’s adoption. A central variable in determining the future adoption and 
development of stablecoins is the degree to which they are integrated into existing 
payment systems. Greater integration not only increases accessibility for a broader 
range of customers but also improves the ease of use. This is especially relevant as 
stablecoins are still mainly used in the crypto landscape and by digitally savvy users. 
Banks could therefore support the adoption of stablecoins for retail, day-to-day 
payments by integrating the asset into their digital systems or creating their own 
tokens, adding it as a new payment rail. Several examples in the EU and the US show 
the role banks can play in issuing their own stablecoins, defending their place in the 
payment landscape, even when it comes to new technologies. By issuing their own 
stablecoins, banks could also avoid losing client relationships and reduce the risk of 
becoming commodity providers for new payment issuers. Beyond this, banks are also 
exploring ways in which stablecoin integration could improve the customer experience 
while increasing fee revenues. 
Looking specifically at Europe, banks’ stablecoin issuance could also play a significant 
role in the growth of EUR-denominated stablecoins. To date, USD-pegged stablecoins 
represent a striking 99% of all stablecoins in use. The widespread use of USD stablecoins 
in the EU would have significant monetary implications on top of those discussed here 
for the banking sector. Over the past few years, the European Union has increasingly 
focused on its ‘strategic autonomy’ agenda. Part of the discussion concerns the Union’s 
significant reliance on non-EU payment services, primarily US-based ones. The 
development of EUR-denominated stablecoins by local banks could provide an answer 
to these concerns, allowing for the development of a fast, cost-efficient European 
payment system directly competing with non-EU alternatives. 
5

### Page 31

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Potentially significant implications, but banks are unlikely to 
surrender ground to fintech stablecoin issuers 
The broad adoption of stablecoins could have a significant impact on European banks. 
These range from a net drop in retail deposits or a shift towards wholesale funding to 
greater concentration and spillover risks, as well as an overall negative effect on credit 
provision and potentially banks’ business models. 
While it remains important to continue exploring these risks as stablecoin use grows, it’s 
also crucial to highlight that the banking sector has tools at its disposal to not only 
counter them, but also benefit from the underlying technology. Indeed, one should not 
expect the “traditional” financial sector to simply stand aside as crypto assets continue 
to gain traction. 
Despite significant growth over the past five years, stablecoins remain far from reaching 
widespread adoption. In our previous publication (read more here), we identified several 
factors that could shape their future. For banks, the key question is how to compete with 
the benefits stablecoins offer. The sector is already making substantial progress in 
instant-payment technologies and exploring digital alternatives (such as tokenisation) 
that could provide similar benefits with fewer regulatory concerns. These initiatives 
could significantly reduce stablecoins’ relative appeal. Furthermore, it’s essential to 
remember that banks still play a central role in payments and could also play a crucial 
role in the uptake of stablecoins by issuing their own tokens. This includes influencing 
which currency-pegged stablecoins gain traction and how they are integrated into the 
broader financial system. 
Next year promises to deliver another wave of stablecoin-related news. In the EU, the 
MiCAR review could translate into a change in the reserve framework, a key issue when 
assessing the potential impact of stablecoins on banks. In the UK, 2027 will also be an 
important year as it will see the enforcement of its comprehensive stablecoin regime in 
October. The US must also still finalise its CLARITY Act, potentially allowing stablecoin 
issuers to directly distribute remuneration to holders. This would be a world first and a 
major shift in the regulatory treatment of the crypto asset, opening the door to similar 
changes in other countries. 
While the upcoming regulatory changes could alter the potential impact of stablecoin 
adoption on the banking sector, we should not expect banks to surrender ground to 
fintech stablecoin issuers without a fight.

### Page 32

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

On 30 January 2027, the amendments to the Solvency II regime will apply, making 
these revisions one of the important regulatory themes for 2027. One of the key 
objectives of the Solvency II reforms is to release capital and promote long-term 
investments by European insurance companies in support of the ambitions of Europe’s 
Savings and Investments Union (SIU). Such capital release could also increase the 
capacity of insurance companies to invest more in corporate and bank bonds. 
During the past decade, insurance companies have reduced their bond holdings in 
favour of equity. Rather than an active reallocation of assets, this partially reflects 
second-round effects related to the strong equity market growth and the rise in bond 
yields and corresponding decline in bond valuations against the backdrop of the ECB 
rate hikes in 2022 and 2023. Higher yields have also coincided in recent years with a 
stronger focus by banks on issuance in shorter maturities, rather than the longer 
maturities traditionally favoured by insurance companies. This has been particularly 
noteworthy for covered bonds. 
We don’t expect the Solvency II revisions to reverse this trend, particularly as the 
amendments promote long-term equity investments. Instead, for most bank bonds, the 
spread-risk capital treatment remains broadly unchanged. Unsecured and subordinated 
bank bonds continue to be treated as corporate bonds with capital charges determined 
by their ratings and duration, while qualifying covered bonds keep their preferential 
treatment. The impact of the Solvency II revisions therefore arises mainly indirectly 
through lower overall capital requirements, while securitisations are the only debt 
instruments benefiting directly through a more favourable capital treatment. 
Nonetheless, certain reforms are worth highlighting for their potential impact on 
insurance demand for different bank bonds. Among these, the amendments relating to

bank bonds. Securitisations benefit most directly through lower capital charges, while 
covered bonds could benefit modestly from the new liquidity-buffer rules

long-term equity investments, securitisations and the volatility adjustment are 
particularly noteworthy. 
Insurance companies increase equity versus debt exposures (€bn)

Source: EIOPA, ING

Long-term equity investments and non-life liquidity buffers 
Under Solvency II, insurance companies can classify part of their equity holdings as 
long-term equity investments (LTEI), benefiting from a lower 22% capital charge. To 
qualify, these investments must be held for at least five years, be listed in the EEA or 
OECD, meet diversification standards and be managed under a long-term investment 
policy. The insurer must also be able to avoid forced sales for five years, even under 
stressed conditions. 
The Solvency II amended Delegated Regulation introduces two approaches to 
demonstrate that forced equity sales can be avoided. 1) The insurer must either have 
sufficient illiquid homogeneous life insurance liabilities with a duration of more than 9.5 
years or a liquidity buffer exceeding 100% for non-life insurance obligations. 2) The 
insurer can also apply a forced-selling test to prove that it can generate cash inflows 
that are consistently higher than the cash outflows over five years, including under 
stress. 
The liquidity buffer requirements for non-life insurance activities are similar to the 
Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) requirements for banks. The portfolio of liquid assets can 
comprise Level 1, Level 2A and Level 2B assets, with the total value of Level 2A and Level 
2B assets capped at 40% and the value of the Level 2B assets at 15%. 
• Qualifying Level 1 assets, subject to a 0% haircut, are cash, exposures to the ECB, 
multilateral development banks and international organisations, and domestic 
currency exposures to EEA central governments and central banks. 
• Qualifying Level 2A assets, subject to a 15% haircut, are bonds and loans rated AA- 
or better that are not issued by an insurance company or bank, and covered bonds 
rated AA- or better as referred to in Article 3(1) of the European Covered Bond 
Directive (CBD) (ie ‘European Covered Bonds’). 
• Qualifying Level 2B assets, subject to a 25% haircut, are Simple, Transparent and 
Standardised (STS) securitisations (not originated by group entities) rated AA- or 
better or that constitute a senior tranche; or subject to a 50% haircut, bonds and 
loans rated A+ to BBB- that are not issued by an insurance company or bank, and 
equity investments listed in the EEA or OECD, or traded on multilateral trading 
facilities, that are not long-term or strategic equity investments and are not 
investments in an insurance company or bank.

While unsecured bank bonds are excluded as eligible liquid assets, the LTEI-related 
liquidity buffer approach could generate some additional demand for covered bonds or 
STS securitisations of banks. However, we don’t expect this effect to be significant given 
the relatively small share of non-life insurance obligations and insurers’ sizeable existing 
portfolios of other liquid assets, such as government bonds. 
Insurers have limited investments in covered bonds or collateralised securities

Source: EIOPA, ING

The LTEI liquidity approach is separate from the generic liquidity and concentration risk 
management requirements under Solvency II, as well as from the obligation under the 
amended Solvency II Directive to provide for a liquidity risk management plan. 
Lower capital requirements for securitisations 
The amended Solvency II Delegated Regulation lowers the capital requirements for 
securitisation notes. This should encourage insurers to invest more in these transactions, 
helping banks to transfer risk and expand lending. 
• The risk factors (ai and bi) for the spread risk of senior tranches of STS securitisations 
will become fully aligned with the treatment of similarly rated covered bonds as 
referred to in CBD Article 3(1). 
• The risk factors for non-senior STS securitisations are reduced to a similar extent, 
with the new capital charges for AAA-rated non-senior notes at c.70% of the current 
capital charge, and for AA-rated non-senior notes at 75% of the current requirement. 
• The difference in the capital treatment between STS and non-STS securitisations is 
currently far more significant for insurers than for banks. This difference will be 
reduced substantially, while the capital charge remains capped at 100%. 
• The current Solvency II regime makes no distinction between senior and non-senior 
non-STS securitisation notes, exaggerating the spread risk of senior compared with 
non-senior tranches. Hence, lower risk factors are introduced for senior non-STS 
compared to non-senior non-STS transactions. 
As a result of these amendments, the difference in capital requirements between CBD-
compliant covered bonds and senior STS securitisations will disappear from the end of 
January, while in the highest rating buckets (AAA and AA) securitisations will be treated 
better than unsecured bonds or third-country covered bonds. Further down the rating 
scale, STS securitisations will be treated comparably to unsecured bonds from a capital 
perspective, where corporate and bank unsecured bonds now benefit from a more 
favourable capital charge compared to similarly rated senior STS securitisations.

European insurance companies currently hold only about €37bn in collateralised debt, 
such as asset-backed securities (ABS) and residential mortgage-backed securities 
(RMBS). This is only 12% of what they hold in covered bonds and 3% of their corporate 
debt portfolio. 
The Solvency II amendments may motivate insurance companies to diversify their debt 
portfolios a bit further away from covered bonds and other bank bond products towards 
securitisations. However, the impact is not expected to be substantial given that the 
weighted average lives of securitisation notes may not always be well suited to the 
longer-term investment needs of insurance companies. 
Volatility adjustment to align spread sensitivities more closely 
Insurance companies calculate the best estimate of their liabilities by discounting the 
expected future liability cash flows using the relevant risk-free interest rate term 
structure. To mitigate the balance sheet impact of bond spread volatility (ie, on the 
asset side of their balance sheet), insurance companies can discount their liabilities at 
the risk-free rate plus a volatility adjustment (VA).

The volatility adjustment increases the discount rate 
Spot rate, %

Source: EIOPA, ING

For each relevant currency (or national market in the case of the euro area), the 
volatility adjustment represents 65% of the spread (S) between the interest rate on the 
reference portfolio and the relevant risk-free interest rate, adjusted for a risk correction 
(RC). The latter correction factor removes the part of the spread that is attributable to 
expected losses or unexpected credit or other risks to the assets. The volatility 
3

### Page 36

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

adjustment is only added to the applicable risk-free interest rates that are not derived 
from extrapolation. 
For each relevant country, a country-specific adjustment may also be added to the 
volatility adjustment before the application of the 65% factor if a) the risk-corrected 
country spread is more than twice the risk-corrected currency spread, and b) the risk-
corrected country spread is more than 85bp. The country component in the volatility 
adjustment helps mitigate excessive widening in specific countries. 
Under the current regime, the representative currency and country portfolios are 
constructed as uniform portfolios, even though insurers’ actual fixed-income portfolios 
may differ substantially. Some insurers can therefore benefit excessively from the 
volatility adjustment if the resulting reduction in the best estimate of their liabilities 
exceeds the decline in the value of the relevant assets when credit spreads widen. 
For this reason, the Solvency II revisions change the calculation of the volatility 
adjustment by a) making the applicable risk corrections (RC) more spread-sensitive, and 
b) introducing a credit spread sensitivity ratio (CSSR) to better align the volatility 
adjustment with the insurer’s asset-liability profile. The latter ratio reflects the price 
value of a basis point of the insurer’s bond, securitisation and loan investments versus 
the price value of a basis point of the best estimate of liabilities, with a minimum of 0 
and a maximum of 1. 
Moreover, subject to approval by the supervisory authority, an undertaking-specific 
adjustment (USA) of up to 105% may be applied to align the risk-corrected spread on 
the reference portfolio more closely with that of the insurer’s own portfolio. This must 
not lead to an increase in the applied risk-adjusted spread for more than two 
consecutive quarters. Moreover, when the USA is used, the insurer cannot also increase 
the volatility adjustment through a macro volatility adjustment. 
The macro volatility adjustment replaces the existing country-specific adjustment and 
can be added to the euro volatility adjustment when the relevant conditions are met. It 
aims to mitigate the impact of excessive spread widening in individual euro area 
countries and is based on the difference between the risk-corrected euro spread and the 
risk-corrected spread for the relevant country. 
Volatility adjustment calculations

Subscripts cu = currency, co = country, ma = macro; VA = volatility adjustment, S = spread of the reference 
portfolio, RC = risk correction, RCS = risk-corrected spread of the reference portfolio, CSSR = credit spread 
sensitivity ratio, USA = undertaking-specific adjustment. 
Source: European Commission, ING

The introduction of additional minimum conditions, such as supervisory approval and 
the availability of adequate processes to calculate the volatility adjustment, has also 
facilitated the upward revision of the general application ratio (GAR) from 65% to 85%.

### Page 37

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Primarily through the introduction of the credit spread sensitivity ratio (CSSR), the 
volatility adjustment will better reflect the relationship between each insurer’s asset and 
liability sensitivity to changes in the volatility adjustment. The revision may provide 
modest support for bonds that increase the asset portfolio’s spread sensitivity, as a 
higher CSSR results in a larger volatility adjustment until the ratio reaches its cap of one. 
The revised calculations should also reduce the possibility for some insurers to benefit 
disproportionately from the volatility adjustment when their assets are less spread-
sensitive than the reference portfolio. 
Conclusion 
The Solvency II amendments are unlikely to trigger a material reallocation in favour of 
bank bonds. Securitisations are the most obvious beneficiaries of a more favourable 
capital treatment, while covered bonds and unsecured bank bonds should mainly 
benefit indirectly from lower overall capital requirements for insurers. We therefore 
expect the revisions to only support insurer demand for bank bonds at the margin.

Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
10:46 October 16 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September Labour Force Survey. Employment surprised
to the downside, -39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force participation from August’s near-
historic high largely offset. The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August was revised up.

Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless, the take home point from the update is that the

### Page 2

labour market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs below working-age population growth.
We expect the unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter financial conditions and cost-of-
living pressures restrict activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast peak is near at
4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the communication challenge for the RBA. The
September minutes were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing on materialising

upside risks to inflation. But, with the unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption of
4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the labour market is becoming harder to defend.
Still, with a clear focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our near-term CPI forecasts in
the October Market Outlook – we still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in September, taking the annual rate up to

3.7%yr. Energy prices were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation was a touch above
consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr). Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months (0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4%
(3.1%yr) on a 0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport services and a 0.3%
increase in medical care services (2.7%yr and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release of

the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures, with the headline and core measures up
6.3%yr and 5.0%yr respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these developments, firms
across multiple districts citing tariffs, insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost
pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect further price increases as contracts/hedges
roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is enough signal in these reports to warrant a

follow-up hike at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee members believe they are
near the end of the tightening cycle and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman both
showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear bias towards an October or December decision.
Williams stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in this cycle given known information.
And Waller also showed confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are doing some of the

FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating
that one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation near the 2.0%yr medium-term target.
However, as discussed in this month’s Market Outlook, capacity pressures are likely to see inflation
consistently above target, rather than below, in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity
in the housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to remain modestly restrictive over

the forecast horizon and will also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade account. At USD133.5bn, the September
result was a whisker inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More impressively, throughout
2025 and 2026 year-to-date the surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the average of
2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth of export growth is as impressive as the headline

annual gain of 36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese investment in
neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time, both trade and income receipts from offshore
investment should buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound foreign investment should
moderate the pace of gains against other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.

### Page 3

Related articles
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the evolution of producer prices across
Asia, and the likelihood of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent decades
continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was one of the key drivers of declining goods prices.
While still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing development to instead be driven by
capital, technology and energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking production are

also increasingly prioritising resilience, security and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore
more probably future supply-chain developments will help contain rising costs through productivity than
drive sustained price declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.

Document 293: COLA comfort for older consumers_ Older households’ spending has lagged, but the 2027 Social Security cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) could help narrow the gap. 2 min read

13034011     1

Economy 
COLA comfort for older consumers? 
27 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Older generations' spending growth has lagged other cohorts over the last year in Bank of America aggregated credit and debit 
card data, despite sizeable wealth gains. This likely partly reflects the highly uneven distribution of wealth among older 
households, limiting the extent to which rising wealth supports spending across the cohort. 
• More recently, stronger consumer spending appears to be primarily a labor-income story. However, older households are less 
exposed to accelerating wage growth. In addition, large differences in their income sources help explain the considerable 
variation in spending among this cohort. 
• The recently announced 3.6% Social Security cost-of-living adjustment could provide a meaningful boost in 2027. If inflation 
moderates as expected, benefit increases may outpace price growth, supporting older households' purchasing power and 
potentially narrowing their spending gap with other generations.

Older generations’ spending growth has lagged  
While total credit and debit card spending per household has picked up in 2026 in Bank of America internal data, a notable 
laggard has been the spending growth of older generations (Baby Boomers and Traditionalists). For around a year, discretionary 
spending growth for these older generations has been lower than that of overall households. This is in stark contrast to 2022-
2023, when they were significantly outstripping overall household spending growth (Exhibit 1).  
Exhibit 1: Discretionary spending growth has been slower among older (Baby Boomer and Traditionalist) households despite wealth effects 
Total credit and debit card spending per household on discretionary categories by older generations and overall (three-month average, % YoY)

On one level, this observation seems surprising given that in our piece on the older consumer we noted that a large and growing 
proportion of net worth is held by older cohorts (read: Entering the age of the older consumer). So what’s going on? One 
explanation is that wealth is very unevenly distributed even within the older cohort. Exhibit 2 uses the latest Federal Reserve 
(2025) Survey of Consumer Finances to illustrate that, across households older than 65 years of age, around two-thirds of net 
wealth is held by the wealthiest 10% of households. As a result, wealth gains from rising stock markets may not trickle too far 
down the wealth distribution of older consumers.  
Exhibit 2: The wealth distribution for older households is very skewed  
Distribution of net wealth for households over 65 years old (%)

Source: Federal Reserve Board of Governors (2025 Survey of Consumer Finances)  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Income and spending become more diverse and unequal with age 
Another important part of the story is that the rise in overall spending growth over the past year appears to be supported by 
labor market strength as well as rising wealth. The October Consumer Checkpoint described how a pickup in after-tax wage 
growth among lower- and middle-income households was likely a key factor driving a recovery in these cohorts’ spending growth 
(read: Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers). As households age, their income sources become more diverse (Exhibit 3), 
which means older generations will likely benefit less from this pickup in wage growth.  
Exhibit 3: As households age their sources of income become more diverse  
Sources of income by age (%)

Source: Federal Reserve Board of Governors (2025 Survey of Household Economics and Decision making) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
The greater diversity in income sources among older households helps explain the wide dispersion in their total incomes and 
spending. For example, using Bank of America internal data, Exhibit 4 shows that households over age 62 that rely solely on

The 2027 COLA may offer some respite  
Still in our view there are some reasons for optimism around older generations spending outcomes.  
For some older households in the middle of the income distribution, tax policy is likely providing some support to their spending 
growth. The One Big Beautiful Bill Act introduced a temporary deduction of up to $6,000 for each taxpayer aged 65 or older for 
2025-2028, with the benefit phasing out above $75,000 of modified adjusted gross income for single filers and $150,000 for 
joint filers. This measure is likely lifting after-tax income and spending capacity for older households – particularly those in the 
middle of the income distribution.  
However, households on lower incomes may see less benefit from these policies as they likely already owe limited federal 
income tax. For these households relying on Social Security either as their sole or as a large component of their income, the 
forthcoming annual cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) to social security benefits will likely be a more important determinant of 
their budgets for 2027. The announced COLA for 2027 is a rise of 3.6% (Exhibit 7) – the largest since 2023, though lower than 
the 8.7% rise in 2022. 
Exhibit 7: The 2027 COLA increase is the largest since 2023 and close to the historical average  
Social Security cost of living adjustments (% increase as of January effective date)

Source: US Social Security Administration (SSA)   
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
The COLA increases are based on the year-over-year (YoY) % change between the third quarters of successive years in the 
Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers (CPI-W) and are typically announced in October and effective 
the following January. If the COLA increase simply keeps benefits in line with inflation, why is it particularly interesting? For sure, 
many times it is not. But when inflation is close to a turning point, the increase can end up looking relatively high or low 
compared with inflation over the course of the following year. The 2022 COLA increase was a case in point, as inflation in the 
subsequent year slowed sharply. In our view, this was one reason older generations’ spending growth was stronger than that of 
other age cohorts over 2022-2023.   
Exhibit 8: Inflation is expected to decline next year 
Consumer Price Index (CPI) (% YoY), third quarter CPI-W increases (red dots, % YoY) and latest consensus CPI forecast for 2027 (dotted line, % YoY)

Source: Haver Analytics, Bloomberg. Some fall 2025 months might be missing due to the government shutdown.

Regarding the 2027 COLA announcement, there are some parallels with 2022, albeit on a smaller scale. In particular, the 
consensus expectation is that CPI inflation is likely to cool over 2027, largely reflecting the lessening impact of higher gasoline 
prices (Exhibit 8).  
So if these expectations prove correct, households that rely on Social Security may receive a more impactful boost to their 
budgets next year than they have for the last few, potentially narrowing the gap between older generations’ spending and other 
households.  
What categories of spending growth might benefit from this? It is hard to be sure, but notable growth deficits for older 
generations currently reside in discretionary areas like electronics, lodging and restaurants (Exhibit 9). Some of these may reflect 
the preferences of older households, but it could be that there is room for some narrowing in these areas.  
Exhibit 9: Large spending growth gaps for older households exist in a range of discretionary goods and services

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Bank of America Proprietary Market Landscape Insights Study is an online quantitative survey among Bank of America customers and 
noncustomers sampled and balanced to provide a representative view of the U.S. adult population. Insights are based on aggregated and 
anonymized responses to surveys. Significance testing is done at the 90 percent confidence interval. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL:
Government Bond Yields Ease,
Gold Falls
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 1 Nov 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Gaël Gaborel - OrbisTerrae / Unsplash
Investors sought safety in government bonds last week as the AI selloff
rattled stock markets worldwide. Yields eased across most of the major
bond markets. The stock market selloff has been hardest on the markets
that soared the most this year. October is ending with the US well ahead
of the rest of the world. That validates our recent shift to market weight on
the Go Global portfolio strategy. Here’s more:

I. Stock Market Performance
The Stay Home vs Go Global ratios have rebounded sharply since mid-
year, driven by US outperformance (chart). They remain below their early
2025 peaks but are heading back up.
The US MSCI’s share of the All Country World MSCI’s market cap has
risen back to 65.3% (chart). Its share of forward earnings is 54.3%. That
11-point gap is the widest since January 2025. US stocks again are
pricing in far more than their share of global earnings.

### Page 3

October’s biggest losers were this year’s AI winners. South Korea finished
the month at the bottom of the country ETF rankings, down 16.4% in
dollars (chart). Taiwan fell 10.4%. Switzerland topped the board with an
8.8% gain, followed by Mexico (4.8%). The US slipped 0.9%, while the All
Country World ex-US ETF fell 2.9%.
II. Stock Markets Earnings & Valuation

### Page 4

Overseas forward earnings momentum is the strongest in over 15 years.
Forward earnings for the All Country World ex-US MSCI is up 41.7% y/y,
topping its 2021 post-pandemic peak (chart). Forward revenues is up
11.6% y/y. Earnings outpacing revenues by that much implies forward
profit margins are widening significantly.
Japan leads the major markets in forward earnings growth since early
2009 (chart). Its forward earnings has risen 720.7% in yen. The US is up

524.6%. EM forward earnings has surged this year and is now up 224.7%
over the same period. The EMU and the UK have barely doubled.
Valuation multiples are falling even as earnings soar (chart). The US
MSCI’s forward P/E has dropped to 19.1 from 23.7 a year ago. Japan’s is

### Page 5

14.7, and the EMU’s is 13.9. The UK trades at 11.9. EM trades at 8.9, its
lowest since the 2008 financial crisis.
III. Fixed Income
Government bond yields eased last week in most major markets in
response to the AI selloff (chart). The US 10-year Treasury yield has fallen
to 4.91% from 5.00% a week earlier. France’s 10-year government bond
yield has eased to 4.98%. The UK’s 5.30% and Australia’s 5.14% remain
the highest in the group. Japan’s yield has dropped to 2.76%.

IV. Gold
We've been bullish on the gold price since it jumped to a new record high
above $2,000 per ounce in early 2024. We reckoned that buying by the

### Page 6

central banks of countries that feared the US and Europe might freeze
their forex reserves, as happened to Russia after it invaded Ukraine,
which would be bullish for gold. That was true until the war started in the
Middle East on February 28, 2026 (chart).
We aren't sure why gold hasn't bounced off of its key support levels.
Instead, it dropped back below $4,000 last week. The next support is
around $3,400. Rising interest rates may explain why gold has been so

weak if central bank buying is being swamped by selling from private
investors.
The People's Bank of China is still buying gold (chart). Chinese investors
have lost lots of money in their property and stock markets. Now, they
may be bailing out of gold.

### Page 7

Is there still a case for gold to reach a price of $10,000 per ounce by the
end of the decade? Yes, but the case is getting weaker. If the S&P 500
reaches 10,000 by the end of the decade, investors might rebalance their
portfolios by allocating more to gold (chart).
V. Focus On China & Brazil
China’s MSCI stock price index has fallen to 66.4 in dollars, down from
92.0 at its 2025 high (chart). The property bust and deflation continue to
weigh on the market. So does the rapidly aging population.

Chinese tech has missed the AI boom entirely (chart). The China
Technology ETF (CQQQ) has fallen to 37.78, 65.2% below its February
2021 record high. The Nasdaq-100 ETF (QQQ) is 9.5% below its record
high. We continue to favor EM ex-China over China.

### Page 8

In Brazil, President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva won the October 25 runoff
with nearly 52% of valid votes after trailing Senator Flávio Bolsonaro in the
first round. Investors had positioned for a Bolsonaro win, and Bolsonaro
says he will challenge the result in court. The Brazil MSCI stock price
index fell to 3,508.86 in local currency, giving back most of its post-first-
round gains (chart). It is now up just 1.0% ytd in local currency and 2.2%
in dollars.

Document 352: Kicking and screaming_ Child care costs persist Child care remains a constraint for many families’ budgets, with the average share of monthly child care 8% of a household’s pay. 2

Source PDF: all report pdf/Kicking and screaming_ Child care costs persist Child care remains a constraint for many families’ budgets, with the average share of monthly child care 8% of a household’s pay. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

13030323     1

Economy 
Kicking and screaming: Child care costs persist 
20 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Child care remains a growing financial burden for many households, even as growth in the median payment has moderated, 
according to Bank of America payments data. More families are paying more than $2,000 per month for care, suggesting 
affordability pressures are becoming increasingly concentrated among households facing the highest expenses. 
• Child care cost pressures vary significantly across the country. Nashville and Jacksonville, FL posted the fastest year-over-year 
growth among major metro areas, while New York and Washington DC saw the biggest decrease in median payment growth, 
according to Bank of America payments data. 
• Limited supply may continue to constrain access to child care for some households. Child care employment fell 0.7% year-over-
year in September, suggesting provider capacity remains under pressure and could continue to affect household finances and 
labor force participation.

Child care costs outpace rent and medical services 
Child care remains a significant expense for many families. In September, the growth in child care and other school-related fees 
surpassed other major household expenses such as rent and medical services, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) 
(Exhibit 1). 
Yet, while child care prices continue to rise in official inflation data, median household payments have largely moderated since 
February, according to Bank of America data (Exhibit 2). More importantly, the growth in the number of households paying for 
child care has fallen since the start of 2025. 
Exhibit 1: The cost of child care has surpassed other services such as 
rent in the consumer price index

1.0% year-over-year (YoY) in September but the number of 
households paying for child care fell 1.0% YoY

Fewer lower-income households are paying for child care 
Why is Bank of America payment growth trending in the opposite direction of the Consumer Price Index? Households are likely 
changing the amount of care they use, which is reflected in slower growth in the number paying for child care. As more

The decline in the number of households paying for child care may also reflect families seeking alternatives to formal care 
arrangements. Some may be relying more heavily on relatives, shifting parents’ work schedules or dropping out of the workforce 
altogether to care for their children in response to elevated costs. This could explain why, among all income groups paying for 
care, the number of lower-income households has seen the biggest decline, falling 2.0% year-over-year (YoY) in September 
(Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: The number of lower-income households paying for child care has fallen the most, down 2.0% YoY in September 
Number of households with child care payments (monthly, three-month moving average, YoY%)

More families face larger monthly bills 
Yet even as fewer households pay for child care, the share of those shelling out more than $2,000 per month has increased 
substantially (Exhibit 4). In fact, it has more than doubled since 2023, reaching 7.7% in 2026 year-to-date. Meanwhile, the share 
of those paying $1,000 or less has declined, suggesting higher monthly payments are becoming more common even as the 
national median grows more slowly. 
Exhibit 4: The share of households paying >$2000 a month for child care has more than doubled since 2023 
Monthly child care payments by amount (annual average, share of households, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Rising costs take a bigger bite out of households’ paychecks 
What makes child care “affordable”? According to the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, child care is considered 
affordable if it accounts for no more than 7% of family income. Yet, in Bank of America data, the average child care cost was 8% 
in September.  
While an 8% share may not appear dramatically above the federal affordability benchmark of 7%, it represents an average across 
households’ income levels. For families in high-cost metro areas or those paying more than $2,000 per month, the burden is 
likely considerably higher.

Where child care costs are climbing 
Child care pressures also differ substantially by location. In Bank of America payments data, Nashville posted the fastest YoY 
median payment growth in September, followed by Jacksonville, FL and Phoenix (Exhibit 6). This dispersion of growth across the 
country suggests child care cost pressures are no longer confined to historically expensive coastal markets.  
Interestingly, New York and Washington DC saw the sharpest decline in growth this year. Why might this be? The supply side of 
the market may help explain why – for example, New York City recently introduced universal Pre-K.1

Fewer providers, higher costs 
As of September 2026, employment in child care services was down 0.7% from a year earlier, suggesting the sector’s workforce 
recovery has stalled. Fewer workers can constrain the number of available places or the hours providers offer. That may leave 
families facing longer searches, fewer workable arrangements or continued price pressure. In turn, access to care can influence 
parents’ ability to enter, remain in or expand their participation in the workforce. 
Exhibit 7: Employment within child care services fell 0.7% YoY in September 
Employees by sector (monthly, seasonally adjusted, YoY%)

Source: Haver Analytics  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
What are the economic implications of rising child care costs? 
Child care costs may have important labor market implications for families with young children. Of those households paying for 
child care in 2026, more than half of them were Older Millennials (52%), followed by Younger Millennials (20%) and Gen X (16%), 
according to Bank of America payments data. This means child care costs weigh more heavily on Older Millennials. 
The share of Older Millennial households receiving more than one paycheck has continued to grow from a year earlier, but the 
pace of that improvement has moderated over the past three months (Exhibit 8). Given that this group accounts for the majority 
of households paying for child care in Bank of America payments data, they may offer early signs of how affordability affects 
their employment decisions. 
Exhibit 8: The share of Older Millennial households paying for child care that have more than one person with a paycheck has declined since 2021  
Share of households with a child care payment with more than one person with a paycheck (%, monthly, three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Child care challenges can weigh on women’s employment 
According to data from the American Time Use Survey, women continue to perform significantly more child care and household 
work than men, even as their participation in paid work has increased. This suggests that persistent gaps in access and 
affordability may have disproportionate consequences for women’s employment. Indeed, the percentage of women who cited 
child care as a reason for temporary unemployment was nearly three times higher than men’s in 2026, though the average fell 
from 2025, according to data from the BLS. 
Yet labor force participation data indicates a more nuanced picture. Despite persistent child care pressures, mothers are 
participating in the workforce at rates that remain above their 2019 averages.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Bank of America credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card holders 
making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards is excluded. Data regarding 
merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial services 
companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS), 
which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
If applicable, the consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level.  
If applicable, any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a 
deposit account or credit card. Any reference to aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-
peer, cash and checks.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Westpac New Zealand Economics
Following on?
• We expect the RBNZ to raise the OCR by 25bps
to 3% at the 28 October Review.
• A vote seems very possible as differences in
view will likely remain among the MPC about
how much the medium-term inflation outlook
has really shifted in recent weeks.
• Data since September show a higher outlook
for year-end inflation and a stronger GDP
starting point, but increased risks to domestic
consumption and residential investment. On

balance, the medium-term inflation picture
will probably be judged to have worsened
slightly given recent movements in energy
prices and the exchange rate.
• Forward guidance will likely leave open
the option to raise the OCR further at the
December Monetary Policy Statement without
presenting it as a sure thing.
• Given the ‘pause’ signal in September, the
press release will likely emphasise the more
concerning short-term inflation picture driven

by the lower exchange rate and higher energy
prices. The tightening in financial conditions
through higher short- and long-term interest
rates, as well as lingering spare capacity, will
be presented as offsets that means the MPC
retains confidence that inflation will return
towards 2% over time.
• We still think a more measured tightening
approach is appropriate from here. Raising the
OCR at every meeting until something ‘breaks’
could be a risky strategy if downside growth

concerns crystalise.
• Nonetheless, the expected hike will bring the
OCR to the top of the RBNZ’s neutral range
more quickly. We now think the OCR will hit
3.5% a meeting earlier in February. Thereafter
we expect further 25bp hikes at the June and
September 2027 meetings, lifting the OCR to a
peak of 4%.

We expect the RBNZ to raise the OCR by 25bps to 3% at

Preview of RBNZ October 2026
Monetary Policy Review

Westpac New Zealand Economics
outlook, the OCR may need to increase further (the
September projection had portrayed an OCR eventually
rising to 3.25%). The RBNZ will emphasise the future OCR
path is not predetermined.
In September, the MPC agreed that further tightening in
policy was likely required. The bulk of the MPC saw upside
risks to the inflation outlook while two members (Conway
and Hansen) thought the risks were balanced. Recent
developments will likely not have made those seeing

upside risks to inflation more comfortable, while the
more dovish MPC members will likely still see the growth
downside risks given continued softness in house prices
and the mounting pressure on real incomes. Indeed, all
MPC members saw some degree of downside risks to
growth which likely remain relevant. All these messages
should remain in the RBNZ’s updated views.
The MPC will likely continue to present their decision as a
gradual evolution of the approach taken since July, where

a higher OCR now is insurance against a much higher OCR
later should upside medium-term inflation risks arise. We
doubt the objective will be to push market interest rates
materially higher.
The exchange rate will likely have greater prominence
at this Review, as it did at the July meeting. The TWI is
just over 3% lower than previously assumed which – if
sustained – will add some inflation pressure over the
next few quarters. We suspect this will not have shifted

the RBNZ’s medium term outlook for inflation. So, the
discussion will seek to link the implications of the lower
exchange rate for short term inflation projections with the
risk this spills over into medium term inflation. We doubt
the RBNZ will be trying to actively support the exchange
rate in any meaningful way, and indeed the lower
exchange rate may be presented as something supportive
of the medium-term growth profile, reducing risks there.
Financial conditions more broadly are likely to receive

some attention. While the exchange rate is lower than
assumed, there have also been marked increases in
wholesale long-term interest rates and (to a lesser extent)
mortgage rates. The impact of higher interest rates on
managing medium term inflation risks will likely be noted.
The RBNZ is likely to continue to emphasise that at this
stage there is little evidence of second-round inflation
impacts coming from the Iran war and the associated rise
in energy prices. We note that most other central banks

are drawing a similar judgement at this point. Today’s CPI
might be consistent with that judgement, although core
inflation measures have increased and will likely be noted
by the more hawkish members of the MPC. We expect the
RBNZ to point to the risks that such persistent inflation
pressures might emerge at some point. But that would
depend on the duration of the oil price shock. We expect
the RBNZ to continue to have the assumption that the
shock dissipates and that energy prices and hence CPI

inflation will be appreciably lower in the medium term.
